What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself. I keep thinking maybe I should be handling it better by now, but yeah… it’s been around three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities that you normally found pleasurable before this incident?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can still do them, like I’ll sit with my husband or put on one of my shows after work, but it doesn’t feel the same. I know those things should feel comforting, or maybe make me a little happy, and I keep waiting for that to come back, but it’s just kind of… blank. I’m trying not to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… guilty? I don’t know. I mean, I keep turning it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I guess I wonder if I should’ve done something different, but I don’t want to make it sound like I think it was all on me or anything. Is that… kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive since the accident?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t think so. I’ve felt really shaken and kind of not like myself, but I haven’t been wishing I wasn’t alive or anything like that. I hope that’s okay to say plainly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m trying to handle it and not be dramatic, but when I’m anywhere near receiving now, I’m constantly checking the racks and how high the pallets are, where the exits are, who’s moving behind me… like I can’t really trust the floor to be safe for even a minute. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I’ve really noticed. My clothes seem to fit about the same, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself or anything, so I don’t want to say for sure, but I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m tired after the late shift, so I think I should just fall asleep, but most nights when I’m starting to drift off, I kind of jerk awake like I heard pallets shifting or something. And even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve noticed, anyway. I mean, I’m tired sometimes, so maybe I’m not as quick as usual, but I wouldn’t say I’m moving really slowly or anything. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really fidgety, I guess. I mean, I might shift around a bit if I’m nervous, but I haven’t noticed myself pacing or anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're doing things more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I might be a little tired or distracted sometimes, but I haven’t noticed that I’m doing things slower than I used to. Not really. Is that okay—like, is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. My clothes don’t feel especially different or anything. I mean, I haven’t really been keeping track of my weight, but I haven’t noticed a big change. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the accident at work over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it does pop into my head. Like out of nowhere I’ll kind of see that pallet coming down again, or hear the crash, especially if a forklift beeps or someone drops something at work. I keep thinking maybe I should be handling it better by now, but yeah… it’s been around three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities that you normally found pleasurable before this incident?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can still do them, like I’ll sit with my husband or put on one of my shows after work, but it doesn’t feel the same. I know those things should feel comforting, or maybe make me a little happy, and I keep waiting for that to come back, but it’s just kind of… blank. I’m trying not to make too much of it, but that’s honestly part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… guilty? I don’t know. I mean, I keep turning it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I guess I wonder if I should’ve done something different, but I don’t want to make it sound like I think it was all on me or anything. Is that… kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive since the accident?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t think so. I’ve felt really shaken and kind of not like myself, but I haven’t been wishing I wasn’t alive or anything like that. I hope that’s okay to say plainly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m trying to handle it and not be dramatic, but when I’m anywhere near receiving now, I’m constantly checking the racks and how high the pallets are, where the exits are, who’s moving behind me… like I can’t really trust the floor to be safe for even a minute. I know that probably sounds like a lot, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I’ve really noticed. My clothes seem to fit about the same, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself or anything, so I don’t want to say for sure, but I don’t think there’s been a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any trouble sleeping since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m tired after the late shift, so I think I should just fall asleep, but most nights when I’m starting to drift off, I kind of jerk awake like I heard pallets shifting or something. And even when I do sleep, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve noticed, anyway. I mean, I’m tired sometimes, so maybe I’m not as quick as usual, but I wouldn’t say I’m moving really slowly or anything. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really fidgety, I guess. I mean, I might shift around a bit if I’m nervous, but I haven’t noticed myself pacing or anything like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're doing things more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I might be a little tired or distracted sometimes, but I haven’t noticed that I’m doing things slower than I used to. Not really. Is that okay—like, is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. My clothes don’t feel especially different or anything. I mean, I haven’t really been keeping track of my weight, but I haven’t noticed a big change. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the accident at work over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it does pop into my head. Like out of nowhere I’ll kind of see that pallet coming down again, or hear the crash, especially if a forklift beeps or someone drops something at work. My stomach just kind of turns when it happens. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're doing daily tasks with less energy than before the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’m still getting things done, mostly. I wouldn’t say I have no energy or anything like that. Maybe I feel kind of worn out some days, but I don’t know if it’s really less energy than before or just me paying more attention to it now. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.
